Why is it easier to predict the epidemic curve than to reconstruct the underlying contact network?
Authors: Dániel Keliger, Illés Horváth
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We study the deterministic Susceptible-Infected-Susceptible (SIS) epidemic model on weighted graphs. van Mieghem et al. have shown that it is possible to learn an estimated network from a finite time sample of the trajectories of the dynamics that in turn can give an accurate prediction beyond the sample time range, even though the estimated network might be qualitatively far from the ground truth. We give a mathematically rigorous derivation for this phenomenon, notably that for large networks, prediction of the epidemic curves is robust, while reconstructing the underlying network is ill-conditioned. Furthermore, we also provide an explicit formula for the underlying network when reconstruction is possible. At the heart of the explanation, we rely on Szemerédi’s weak regularity lemma.

Introduction

One of the main approaches of epidemic modeling is compartment-based system of differential equations where the compartments correspond to geographic locations or age groups in different stages of the infection (Gozzi et al. 2021). Besides the initial conditions, infection and cure rates, arguably, the most challenging parameter to estimate is the underlying contact network on which the spreading phenomenon occurs. Direct methods usually rely on making modeling assumptions or using proxy networks based on social media networks, GPS and mobile data, commuting patterns or questioners (Islam et al. 2014; Ódor et al. 2021), methods which can be potentially intrusive in terms of privacy or give an unfaithful representation.

An alternative paradigm treats network estimation as a learning problem where we first use observations up to some time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_0>0$$\end{document}T0>0 to find the parameters that are most consistent with the epidemic curves thus far, then using said parameters to extrapolate the curve into a future time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T>T_0$$\end{document}T>T0 (Prasse and Mieghem 2022). Surprisingly, while this method yields an estimated network capable of making accurate predictions for a reasonably long time scale, the realized estimate might radically differ from the ground truth alluding to the fact that only partial information of the underlying network is essential in the evolution of the epidemic. In summary, they show that while the reconstruction of the network is hard, predicting the epidemic curves is robust.

In Prasse and Mieghem (2022) the phenomena was demonstrated numerically along with theoretical arguments. In Beaufort et al. (2022) the authors study a slightly different setting where instead of commuting (individuals returning to their home vertex at a given rate (Gozzi et al. 2021)), individuals perform random walks according to a diffusion matrix. They rigorously prove that almost every diffusion matrix from almost every initial condition can be reconstructed from the trajectories and numerically show that said reconstruction can be challenging. Reconstruction of the background network graphon based on the distribution of branching processes was examined in Hladký et al. (2024). Murphy et al. (2024) provides a different setup with a stochastic process on a random graph, with an entropy inequality implying a tradeoff between prediction and reconstruction. Intuitively, if the evolution of the epidemic is sensitive to the details of the network, then one can extract a rich picture from the trajectories, while prediction becomes hard as even slight perturbations of the network results in a drastic change of dynamic. On the other hand, if many realizations of the random graph give similar trajectories, extrapolation becomes more feasible.

In the present paper, we follow the setting of Prasse and Mieghem (2022) for the Susceptible-Infected-Susceptible (SIS) model and give a proper mathematical setup and rigorous proof that shows that the prediction problem is robust, while the reconstruction problem is ill-conditioned for large networks. To the best of our knowledge, these results are the first in this endeavor. Furthermore, for some cases we also give an explicit formula to reconstruct the underlying network, using only observable quantities (quantities that can be observed or measured directly).

We believe the concepts and methods are robust for generalization to other kinds of models; an important notion is the observable quantities, and irregularities of the evolution also potentially play a role. These could be examined on a case-by-case basis; in the present paper, we do not pursue a general framework.

The rest of the paper is structured as follows: In Section 2 we give an introduction of the SIS model and rigorously define the reconstruction and prediction problems. In Section 3 we state the main results. Section 4 introduces concepts and notation regarding graphons (Lovász 2012) which are crucial for the proofs but non-essential in terms of stating the problems and the corresponding theorems. Section 5 contains the proofs for the main results.

Setup

In this section we give a precise formulation of the model and the problems of interest.

SIS model definition

We study the deterministic SIS metapopulation model. For simplicity of presentation, we choose to focus on SIS model, but most of the results can be extended to a wider class of epidemic models including the SIR or SEIR processes with appropriate modifications (e.g. to the set of observable quantities).

Assume there are n communities represented by the vertex set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V= \{1, \dots , n \}$$\end{document}V={1,⋯,n}. The graph is equipped with both symmetric edge weights \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{ij}=w_{ji}\ge 0$$\end{document}wij=wji≥0 forming the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W:= \left( w_{ij}\right) _{i,j \in V}$$\end{document}W:=wiji,j∈V. Symmetry is usually assumed for similar epidemic models, but is actually not necessary for most of the calculations.

Positive vertex weights \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _{i} \ge 0$$\end{document}πi≥0 are also given with normalization\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{i=1}^{n} \pi _i =1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑i=1nπi=1.The vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi := \left( \pi _{i} \right) _{i \in V}$$\end{document}π:=πii∈V is referred to as the distribution of the population between communities. We also use the matrix notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi := \operatorname {diag} \left( \pi \right) $$\end{document}Π:=diagπ.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{ij}$$\end{document}wij represents the strength of interaction between communities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i,j \in V$$\end{document}i,j∈V. It can also be proportional to the edge density if the weighted graph corresponds to a coarse grained version of a simple graph. Global constants like the infection rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β can also be absorbed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{ij}$$\end{document}wij.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _i \ge 0$$\end{document}γi≥0 denotes the curing rate of community \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in V$$\end{document}i∈V; the corresponding vector notation is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma := \left( \gamma _i \right) _{i \in V}.$$\end{document}γ:=γii∈V. Inhomogeneity of the curing rate might be an important modeling aspect when communities correspond to age groups or other biological characteristics of the population that can affect the curing rate.

The special case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =0$$\end{document}γ=0 is referred to as the SI model.

In the SIS model, individuals can occupy one of two states: susceptible and infected. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_i(t)$$\end{document}zi(t) stand for the ratio of infected individuals at community \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in V$$\end{document}i∈V at time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \ge 0.$$\end{document}t≥0.

Due to the cure rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _i$$\end{document}γi the ratio \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_i(t)$$\end{document}zi(t) is decreased at rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _{i} z_{i}(t) $$\end{document}γizi(t). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_i(t)$$\end{document}zi(t) is increased when a susceptible individual from community i makes an interaction with an infected individual from some community j. Assuming that the communities are well-mixed, the probability that the vertex from community i is susceptible and the vertex from community j is infected is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1-z_i(t))z_{j}(t)$$\end{document}(1-zi(t))zj(t). Finally, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{ij} \pi _{j}$$\end{document}wijπj is proportional to the rate at which such interaction accrues regardless of the state of individuals. That creates a total influx of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sum _{j \in V} w_{ij} \pi _{j} (1-z_{i}(t))z_{j}.(t)$$\end{document}∑j∈Vwijπj(1-zi(t))zj.(t)

The dynamics is given by the following system of ODEs:1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t} z_{i}(t)=(1-z_{i}(t)) \sum _{j \in V} w_{ij} \pi _{j} z_{j}(t)-\gamma _{i} z_{i}(t) {\qquad (i\in V)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtzi(t)=(1-zi(t))∑j∈Vwijπjzj(t)-γizi(t)(i∈V).The initial conditions are assumed to be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le z_i(0) \le 1$$\end{document}0≤zi(0)≤1 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in V$$\end{document}i∈V. Since the right hand side of (1) is locally Lipschitz-continuous, (1) has a unique solution. Furthermore, standard arguments show that the region \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0,1]^n$$\end{document}[0,1]n is positive invariant (Delmas et al. 2020), that is,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0 \le z_i(0) \le 1 \quad \forall i \in V \quad \Longrightarrow \quad 0 \le z_i(t) \le 1 \quad \forall t \ge 0 \,\forall i \in V, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0≤zi(0)≤1∀i∈V⟹0≤zi(t)≤1∀t≥0∀i∈V,so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_i(t)$$\end{document}zi(t) can indeed be interpreted as a ratio.

Using the notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} The system (1) is intimately related to the stochastic version of the SIS process on sufficiently dense simple graphs. Fix the parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n, W, \pi , \gamma ,$$\end{document}n,W,π,γ, and generate a random graph on N vertices where two vertices from communities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i,j \in V$$\end{document}i,j∈V respectively are connected with probability (proportional to) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa ^N w_{ij}$$\end{document}κNwij, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa ^N$$\end{document}κN is a density parameter such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa ^N \gg \frac{ \log N}{ N}.$$\end{document}κN≫logNN. Then, according to Keliger et al. (2022), the ratio of infected individuals in community \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in V$$\end{document}i∈V will stochastically converge to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_{i}(t)$$\end{document}zi(t) uniformly on any compact time interval [0, T]. (Note that although the setting in Keliger et al. (2022) does not allow inhomogeneous \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _i$$\end{document}γi parameters directly, one can augment the state space to consist of pairs (i, S), (i, I) for different \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in V$$\end{document}i∈V. Since n is fixed, the results in Keliger et al. (2022) remain valid for this state space as well.)

We point out that based on the Cauchy–Kovalevskaya theorem, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_i(t)$$\end{document}zi(t) is also an analytic function of t as the right hand side of (1) is itself analytic. This property will be frequently exploited during the proofs, starting with the following lemma.

Lemma 2.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h: [0,\infty [ \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}h:[0,∞[→R be an analytic function. Assume for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_0>0$$\end{document}T0>0 we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\forall t \in [0,T_0] \ h(t)=0$$\end{document}∀t∈[0,T0]h(t)=0. Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\forall t \ge 0 \ h(t)=0.$$\end{document}∀t≥0h(t)=0.

The short proof can be found in Section 5.1.

We will make use of the Gram matrix4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_{ij}(T):= \int _{0}^{T}z_{i}(t) z_{j}(t) \textrm{d}t {\qquad (T>0).} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Gij(T):=∫0Tzi(t)zj(t)dt(T>0).It is symmetric, positive semi-definite, hence it induces a semi-scalar product\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert v \Vert _{G(T)}^2:= \langle v, G(T) v \rangle =\int _{0}^{T} \langle v, z(t) \rangle ^2 \textrm{d}t, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖v‖G(T)2:=⟨v,G(T)v⟩=∫0T⟨v,z(t)⟩2dt,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdot ,\cdot \rangle $$\end{document}⟨·,·⟩ is the standard scalar product on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}Rn.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert v \Vert _{G(T)}$$\end{document}‖v‖G(T) is zero on the nullspace of G(T), denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}.$$\end{document}N. Lemma 2.1 applied for the analytic function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{{v}}(t):=\langle v, z(t) \rangle $$\end{document}hv(t):=⟨v,z(t)⟩ implies that for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<T_0 \le T$$\end{document}0<T0≤T the nullspaces are the same, hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}$$\end{document}N does not depend on T as long as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T>0$$\end{document}T>0.

We will also use the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^r(V,\pi )$$\end{document}Lr(V,π) norm:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert v \Vert _{r,\pi }:= \left( \sum _{i \in V} \left| v_i \right| ^r \pi _i\right) ^{1/r}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖v‖r,π:=∑i∈Vvirπi1/r.Besides the induced matrix norm \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _{p, \pi }$$\end{document}‖·‖p,π, we will also use the so-called cut norm defined as5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert W \Vert _{\square }:= \max _{S,S' \subseteq V} \left| \sum _{ \begin{array}{c} i \in S \\ j \in S' \end{array}}w_{ij }\pi _i \pi _j \right| . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖W‖□:=maxS,S′⊆V∑i∈Sj∈S′wijπiπj.

The reconstruction problem

The setup for the reconstruction problem is the following. We have perfect knowledge of z(t) over a time interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0,T_0]$$\end{document}[0,T0] (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_0>0$$\end{document}T0>0), along with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma .$$\end{document}γ. The task is to find W. Reconstruction is impossible when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_0=0$$\end{document}T0=0 as W can be chosen independently of q, hence, the reason for assumption \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_0>0$$\end{document}T0>0.

In practice, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π being available is a reasonable assumption since it only concerns the size of communities. Knowledge about \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ can be interpreted in several ways: it could be experimentally measured during clinical trials, or a different approach would be to assume that we do not only observe the total change of z(t) over time, but we also observe the gains \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( \frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t} z_{i}(t) \right) _{+}:= (1-z_{i}(t)) \left( \mathcal {A}z(t) \right) _{i}$$\end{document}ddtzi(t)+:=(1-zi(t))Az(t)i and losses \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( \frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t} z_{i}(t) \right) _{-}:=-\gamma _{i}z_{i}(t)$$\end{document}ddtzi(t)-:=-γizi(t) separately, corresponding to new infections and recoveries respectively. From these, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _i$$\end{document}γi can be expressed as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \gamma _{i}=- \frac{1}{z_{i}(t)} \left( \frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t}z_{i}(t) \right) _{-}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}γi=-1zi(t)ddtzi(t)-.We note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}z(t)$$\end{document}Az(t) is also an observable quantity since it has the form of6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \mathcal {A}z(t) \right) _{i}= \frac{\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t}z_{{i}}(t)+\gamma _iz_{i}(t)}{1-z_{i}(t)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Az(t)i=ddtzi(t)+γizi(t)1-zi(t).We set some technical restrictions on the parameters. In general, we want the weights \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{ij}$$\end{document}wij and the curing rates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _i$$\end{document}γi to be bounded from above, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_i(t)$$\end{document}zi(t) to be bounded away from 1 (as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-z_i(t)$$\end{document}1-zi(t) appears in the denominator of the right hand side of (6)). We also want \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_i(0), \pi _i$$\end{document}zi(0),πi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{ij}$$\end{document}wij to be bounded away from 0 to ensure a lower bound on the speed of the disease spreading (especially for the SI case). Accordingly, for bounds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 <m \le 1/2$$\end{document}0<m≤1/2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${M>0}$$\end{document}M>0, we define the parameter sets7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \mathcal {Q}_{m,M}^{(n)}:=&\Bigg \{(z(0),\gamma ,\pi ) \in \mathbb {R}^n \times \mathbb {R}^n \times \mathbb {R}^n | \forall i \in V \ m \le z_{i}(0) \le 1-m, \\&0 \le \gamma _i \le M, 0\le \pi _i, \sum _{j\in V} \pi _j =1 \Bigg \}, \end{aligned}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}Qm,M(n):={(z(0),γ,π)∈Rn×Rn×Rn|∀i∈Vm≤zi(0)≤1-m,0≤γi≤M,0≤πi,∑j∈Vπj=1},8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {P}_{m,M}^{(n)}:=&\left\{ (W,q) \in \mathbb {R}^{n \times n} \times \mathcal {Q}_{m,M}^{(n)} \big | W^{\intercal }=W,\, \forall i,j \in V \, m \le w_{ij} \le M \right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pm,M(n):=(W,q)∈Rn×n×Qm,M(n)|W⊺=W,∀i,j∈Vm≤wij≤M.

Remark 2.2

For a fixed n, the assumption \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m \le w_{ij} \le M$$\end{document}m≤wij≤M is not too restrictive, but later on it will be used as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<m \le M$$\end{document}0<m≤M fixed, essentially making the graph sequence dense, enabling us to apply the theory of graphons in section 4. See also the proofs of Theorems 3.9 and 3.12.

For the reconstruction problem, we will always assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _i>0\, \forall i \in V$$\end{document}πi>0∀i∈V as the dynamics is insensitive to vertices with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _i=0$$\end{document}πi=0 (so reconstruction for those parts of the graph would be impossible anyway). Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _i=0$$\end{document}πi=0 is not an issue for the prediction problem (see Section 2.3), where it is convenient to allow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _i=0$$\end{document}πi=0 as n is allowed to increase.

Since (1) is well-posed, the parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p \in \mathcal {P}_{m,M}^{(n)} $$\end{document}p∈Pm,M(n) uniquely determine the trajectories \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \left( z(t) \right) _{0 \le t \le T_0}$$\end{document}z(t)0≤t≤T0, hence we may define the mapping\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi _{T_0}(p): \ p \mapsto \left( z(t) \right) _{0 \le t \le T_0}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕT0(p):p↦z(t)0≤t≤T0,that maps from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_{m,M}^{(n)}$$\end{document}Pm,M(n) (for some fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<m \le \frac{1}{2}, \ M>0$$\end{document}0<m≤12,M>0) to the space of analytical functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$([0,T_0] \rightarrow [0,1]^n).$$\end{document}([0,T0]→[0,1]n).

Definition 2.3

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=(W, q) \in \mathcal {P}_{m,M}^{(n)}$$\end{document}p=(W,q)∈Pm,M(n), we say W is q-reconstructible if for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_0>0$$\end{document}T0>0, the mapping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _{T_0} \left( \cdot , q \right) $$\end{document}ϕT0·,q is injective at W.

Not all W are reconstructible. For example, assume that the parameter p is regular in the sense that there exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\gamma }, \bar{d}, \bar{z}(0)$$\end{document}γ¯,d¯,z¯(0) such that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in V$$\end{document}i∈V we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_i=\bar{d}, \ \gamma _i=\bar{\gamma }$$\end{document}di=d¯,γi=γ¯ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_{i}(0)=\bar{z}(0).$$\end{document}zi(0)=z¯(0). It is easy to check that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in V$$\end{document}i∈V the solution has the form of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_i(t)=\bar{z}(t)$$\end{document}zi(t)=z¯(t) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{z}(t)$$\end{document}z¯(t) solves\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t}\bar{z}(t)=\bar{d} (1-\bar{z}(t)) \bar{z}(t)-\bar{\gamma }\bar{z}(t). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtz¯(t)=d¯(1-z¯(t))z¯(t)-γ¯z¯(t).However, many other set of parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p \in \mathcal {P}_{m,M}^{(n)}$$\end{document}p∈Pm,M(n) satisfy the regularity assumption with the same triple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${( \bar{\gamma }, \bar{d}, \bar{z}(0))}$$\end{document}(γ¯,d¯,z¯(0)).

The prediction problem

The prediction problem is the following: given q and the curve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( z(t) \right) _{0 \le t \le T_0}$$\end{document}z(t)0≤t≤T0, we aim to predict z(t) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>T_0$$\end{document}t>T0 assuming that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<T_0 < T.$$\end{document}0<T0<T.

One approach to the prediction problem is to find an approximate weighted graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ such that the corresponding solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{z}(t)$$\end{document}z^(t) is close to the original solution z(t) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0,T_0]$$\end{document}[0,T0] according to some error measure, and use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{z}(t)$$\end{document}z^(t) as a prediction for z(t) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>T_0$$\end{document}t>T0. In Prasse and Mieghem (2022) the authors constructed a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ that is consistent with z(t) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0,T_0]$$\end{document}[0,T0] by minimizing the error between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^{\mathcal {A}}(z(t))$$\end{document}FA(z(t)) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^{\hat{\mathcal {A}}}(z(t)).$$\end{document}FA^(z(t)). More precisely, they compute\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\operatorname {argmin}_{\hat{a}_{i,1}, \dots \hat{a}_{i,n}} \sum _{j=1}^{J} \left( \frac{\Delta z_i(t_j)}{\Delta t_j}-F_i^{\hat{\mathcal {A}}}(z(t_j)) \right) ^2+\rho _i \sum _{{k}=1}^{{n}} \hat{a}_{i{k}} \\&s.t. \ \hat{a}_{ij} \ge 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}argmina^i,1,⋯a^i,n∑j=1JΔzi(tj)Δtj-FiA^(z(tj))2+ρi∑k=1na^iks.t.a^ij≥0for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in V,$$\end{document}i∈V, where time is discretized into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0=t_0<t_1<\dots , <t_J=T_0$$\end{document}0=t0<t1<⋯,<tJ=T0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _i \ge 0$$\end{document}ρi≥0 serve as parameters to tune the relaxation terms.

We take a similar approach, but use a different error measure; instead of using the derivatives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^{\mathcal {A}}(z(t))$$\end{document}FA(z(t)) we make use of the neighborhood statistics \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}z(t)$$\end{document}Az(t) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\mathcal {A}}z(t)$$\end{document}A^z(t) (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\mathcal {A}}:=\hat{W} \Pi $$\end{document}A^:=W^Π) as these are also observable and have nicer analytical properties.

We introduce the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^r\left( V, \pi \right) $$\end{document}LrV,π type error terms:9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{r,T}(W,W'):= \left( \int _{0}^{T} \Vert \mathcal {A}z(t)-\hat{\mathcal {A}} z(t) \Vert _{r,\pi }^r \textrm{d}t \right) ^{1/r}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hr,T(W,W′):=∫0T‖Az(t)-A^z(t)‖r,πrdt1/r.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{r,T_0}$$\end{document}Hr,T0 is an observable quantity but \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{r,T}$$\end{document}Hr,T is not when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T>T_0$$\end{document}T>T0, as it depends on the future values of z(t). The following lemma states that if the error \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{2,T}$$\end{document}H2,T is small, then the trajectories generated by W and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ stay close together on [0, T].

Lemma 2.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p, \hat{p} \in \mathcal {P}_{0,M}^{(n)}$$\end{document}p,p^∈P0,M(n) with the corresponding solutions of (1) being z(t) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{z}(t).$$\end{document}z^(t). We have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{0 \le t \le T} \Vert z(t)-\hat{z}(t) \Vert _{1,\pi } \le&\left( \Vert z(0)-\hat{z}(0) \Vert _{1,\pi }+T \Vert \gamma -\hat{\gamma } \Vert +H_{1,T} \right) e^{3MT}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}sup0≤t≤T‖z(t)-z^(t)‖1,π≤‖z(0)-z^(0)‖1,π+T‖γ-γ^‖+H1,Te3MT.Furthermore, assuming \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{z}(0)=z(0)$$\end{document}z^(0)=z(0) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\gamma }=\gamma $$\end{document}γ^=γ, we also have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{0 \le t \le T} \Vert z(t)-\hat{z}(t) \Vert _{1,\pi } \le e^{3MT}H_{1,T} \le \sqrt{T}e^{3MT} H_{2,T}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}sup0≤t≤T‖z(t)-z^(t)‖1,π≤e3MTH1,T≤Te3MTH2,T.

The proof can be found in Section 5.1.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(i)}$$\end{document}η(i) denote the ith row vector of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}-\hat{\mathcal {A}}$$\end{document}A-A^ (that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(i)}_j:= a_{ij}-\hat{a}_{ij}).$$\end{document}ηj(i):=aij-a^ij). Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \left( \mathcal {A}-\hat{\mathcal {A}} \right) z(t) \Vert _{2,\pi }^2=\sum _{i \in V} \langle \eta ^{(i)}, z(t) \rangle ^2 \pi _i, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖A-A^z(t)‖2,π2=∑i∈V⟨η(i),z(t)⟩2πi,implying10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{2,T}^2=\sum _{i \in V} \left\| \eta ^{(i)} \right\| _{G(T)}^2 \pi _i. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H2,T2=∑i∈Vη(i)G(T)2πi.Keeping all the parameters besides \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ fixed, the minimum of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{2,T_0}=H_{2,T_0}(\hat{W})$$\end{document}H2,T0=H2,T0(W^) as a function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ is 0, obtained at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}=W.$$\end{document}W^=W. This means that if we manage to find the minimum of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{2,T_0}$$\end{document}H2,T0 without error, then perfect prediction is possible.

However, this is rarely possible; typically, we target \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{2,T_0}$$\end{document}H2,T0 to be smaller than some given threshold, and the task is then to show continuous dependence of the future error \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{2,T}$$\end{document}H2,T on the past error \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{2,T_0}$$\end{document}H2,T0, that is, we want to show that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon >0$$\end{document}ε>0 there is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta >0$$\end{document}δ>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{2,T_0}<\delta \Rightarrow H_{2,T}<\varepsilon .$$\end{document}H2,T0<δ⇒H2,T<ε.

Results

Results for the reconstruction problem

We start by giving a characterization of q-reconstructability.

Theorem 3.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=(W,q) \in \mathcal {P}_{m,M}^{(n)}$$\end{document}p=(W,q)∈Pm,M(n) and assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _i>0\, \forall i \in V.$$\end{document}πi>0∀i∈V.

W is q-reconstructible.

W is q-reconstructible.

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_0>0$$\end{document}T0>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\forall t \in [0,T_0] \ \langle v, z(t) \rangle {=0} \Rightarrow v=0. $$\end{document}∀t∈[0,T0]⟨v,z(t)⟩=0⇒v=0.

W not being q-reconstructible does not necessarily mean that the counterexample \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{W}$$\end{document}W^ which generates the same trajectory also satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\hat{W},q)\in \mathcal {P}_{m,M}^{(n)}.$$\end{document}(W^,q)∈Pm,M(n). Reconstructability can also be understood in the literal sense, that is, there is an explicit formula for W.
